Hi… good to meet you too. I guess I came in because my worrying has gotten to the point where I can’t really put it away once it starts. Like, if I start worrying about my mother getting up at night, or a grant report I’m responsible for, or whether I missed some small thing at work, it just keeps going in my head.

I make lists—honestly, I make a lot of lists—and I try to tell myself, “Okay, you’ve handled what you can, move on,” but it doesn’t really work. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think that’s a good way to put it. They feel unwanted in the sense that I’ll be trying to do something completely ordinary, like focus on a report at work or just get through the evening, and then the worry sort of appears and takes over. It isn’t like I’m choosing to sit down and think through it carefully. Once it starts—whether it’s about my mother getting up at night, or whether I’ve missed some detail at work, or the grant report—it’s very hard to push it aside.

I’ll make lists, and then sometimes lists about the lists, trying to reassure myself that I’ve covered what I can. But even after that, my mind keeps circling back, like, “Are you sure? What if you forgot something?” So yes, they do feel intrusive, but mostly they feel… sticky, I guess. Like I can’t shelve them, no matter how much I tell myself to move on.

Once it starts—whether it’s about my mother getting up at night, or whether I’ve missed some detail at work, or the grant report—it’s very hard to push it aside.\n\nI’ll make lists, and then sometimes lists about the lists, trying to reassure myself that I’ve covered what I can. But even after that, my mind keeps circling back, like, “Are you sure? What if you forgot something?” So yes, they do feel intrusive, but mostly they feel… sticky, I guess. Like I can’t shelve them, no matter how much I tell myself to move on.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d call it repeating exactly… at least not in a ritual kind of way, if that’s what you mean. I don’t think I’m doing something like touching things a certain number of times or having to redo something in a very specific order.

What I do notice is that I’ll go back over things in my head a lot, and I do check lists or rewrite them because it makes me feel like I’m at least trying to get the worry under control. But I’m not sure where the line is between “being responsible” and “doing it over and over because I’m anxious.” With work things especially, I’ll think, “Let me just make sure I didn’t miss anything,” and then sometimes I’ve spent much longer than I meant to just reviewing the same thing mentally.

So… yes and no, I guess. I do circle back and reassure myself, but I don’t know if it’s a compulsion exactly. It feels more like I’m trying to calm myself down and be certain, and then I can’t quite get the certainty I’m looking for.

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi… good to meet you too. I guess I came in because my worrying has gotten to the point where I can’t really put it away once it starts. Like, if I start worrying about my mother getting up at night, or a grant report I’m responsible for, or whether I missed some small thing at work, it just keeps going in my head.\n\nI make lists—honestly, I make a lot of lists—and I try to tell myself, “Okay, you’ve handled what you can, move on,” but it doesn’t really work. It’s like I can’t shelve the worry in my mind, which is embarrassing to say as a librarian, but that’s exactly what it feels like. It just stays checked out, so to speak, and I keep circling back to it no matter how much I try to redirect myself.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts ever feel intrusive and unwanted, like they pop into your mind even when you don’t want them to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a good way to put it. They feel unwanted in the sense that I’ll be trying to do something completely ordinary, like focus on a report at work or just get through the evening, and then the worry sort of appears and takes over. It isn’t like I’m choosing to sit down and think through it carefully. Once it starts—whether it’s about my mother getting up at night, or whether I’ve missed some detail at work, or the grant report—it’s very hard to push it aside.\n\nI’ll make lists, and then sometimes lists about the lists, trying to reassure myself that I’ve covered what I can. But even after that, my mind keeps circling back, like, “Are you sure? What if you forgot something?” So yes, they do feel intrusive, but mostly they feel… sticky, I guess. Like I can’t shelve them, no matter how much I tell myself to move on.\n\nDoctor: When these worries get stuck, do you find yourself doing anything over and over to feel less anxious, like checking, repeating, or going through something in your mind?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it repeating exactly… at least not in a ritual kind of way, if that’s what you mean. I don’t think I’m doing something like touching things a certain number of times or having to redo something in a very specific order.\n\nWhat I do notice is that I’ll go back over things in my head a lot, and I do check lists or rewrite them because it makes me feel like I’m at least trying to get the worry under control. But I’m not sure where the line is between “being responsible” and “doing it over and over because I’m anxious.” With work things especially, I’ll think, “Let me just make sure I didn’t miss anything,” and then sometimes I’ve spent much longer than I meant to just reviewing the same thing mentally.\n\nSo… yes and no, I guess. I do circle back and reassure myself, but I don’t know if it’s a compulsion exactly. It feels more like I’m trying to calm myself down and be certain, and then I can’t quite get the certainty I’m looking for.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself worrying about many different parts of your life, rather than mainly one specific problem?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 47 / librarian_or_media_collections_specialist / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Excessive_Anxiety_Worry\nmanifestation: \"Over this past year my worrying has spread from just my mother’s safety to finances, staff decisions, deadlines, the house, and whether I’m being a good daughter, and even small things can start to feel like they’re about to unravel.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, definitely. It used to feel more centered on my mother—like whether she was safe at night, whether I should be doing more, whether I’d missed some sign that she needed help. But over this past year it’s spread out, and now it feels like almost any category of my life can turn into something I worry about.

Finances, even if nothing immediate has happened. Staff decisions at work, like whether I handled a situation fairly or said the wrong thing. Deadlines, grant reports, house things I’ve been putting off, repairs, paperwork… and then this whole layer of, “Am I being a good daughter?” That one comes up a lot.

And the frustrating thing is that even small things don’t stay small in my mind. Something like an email I haven’t answered or a bill I need to double-check can start to feel like it’s the first loose thread and everything is about to unravel. I know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it really feels like if I don’t catch every detail, something will go wrong.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think so. I mean, in the moment it feels completely justified, because my mind is telling me, “Well, if you don’t think this through, something bad could happen,” or “If you miss one detail, everything could unravel.” But when I step back, I can see that the level of worry is probably bigger than the actual situation.

Like, it started mostly with my mother’s safety, and that felt understandable to me. But now it’s not just that. It’s finances, staff decisions at work, deadlines, the house, things I need to fix or follow up on, whether I’m being a good daughter… it’s like almost anything can become the thing I latch onto. And the size of the worry doesn’t always match the size of the problem. An unanswered email or a bill I need to double-check can start to feel like some huge warning sign, like if I don’t handle it perfectly, then everything is going to fall apart.

So yes, I do think it’s more than the situation calls for. I don’t always believe that when I’m in it, but afterward I can usually see that my reaction was bigger than what was actually happening.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi… good to meet you too. I guess I came in because my worrying has gotten to the point where I can’t really put it away once it starts. Like, if I start worrying about my mother getting up at night, or a grant report I’m responsible for, or whether I missed some small thing at work, it just keeps going in my head.\n\nI make lists—honestly, I make a lot of lists—and I try to tell myself, “Okay, you’ve handled what you can, move on,” but it doesn’t really work. It’s like I can’t shelve the worry in my mind, which is embarrassing to say as a librarian, but that’s exactly what it feels like. It just stays checked out, so to speak, and I keep circling back to it no matter how much I try to redirect myself.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts ever feel intrusive and unwanted, like they pop into your mind even when you don’t want them to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a good way to put it. They feel unwanted in the sense that I’ll be trying to do something completely ordinary, like focus on a report at work or just get through the evening, and then the worry sort of appears and takes over. It isn’t like I’m choosing to sit down and think through it carefully. Once it starts—whether it’s about my mother getting up at night, or whether I’ve missed some detail at work, or the grant report—it’s very hard to push it aside.\n\nI’ll make lists, and then sometimes lists about the lists, trying to reassure myself that I’ve covered what I can. But even after that, my mind keeps circling back, like, “Are you sure? What if you forgot something?” So yes, they do feel intrusive, but mostly they feel… sticky, I guess. Like I can’t shelve them, no matter how much I tell myself to move on.\n\nDoctor: When these worries get stuck, do you find yourself doing anything over and over to feel less anxious, like checking, repeating, or going through something in your mind?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it repeating exactly… at least not in a ritual kind of way, if that’s what you mean. I don’t think I’m doing something like touching things a certain number of times or having to redo something in a very specific order.\n\nWhat I do notice is that I’ll go back over things in my head a lot, and I do check lists or rewrite them because it makes me feel like I’m at least trying to get the worry under control. But I’m not sure where the line is between “being responsible” and “doing it over and over because I’m anxious.” With work things especially, I’ll think, “Let me just make sure I didn’t miss anything,” and then sometimes I’ve spent much longer than I meant to just reviewing the same thing mentally.\n\nSo… yes and no, I guess. I do circle back and reassure myself, but I don’t know if it’s a compulsion exactly. It feels more like I’m trying to calm myself down and be certain, and then I can’t quite get the certainty I’m looking for.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about many different parts of your life, rather than mainly one specific problem?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Something like an email I haven’t answered or a bill I need to double-check can start to feel like it’s the first loose thread and everything is about to unravel. I know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it really feels like if I don’t catch every detail, something will go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your worrying is more than the situation really calls for?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think part of me knows they’re excessive, even while it’s happening, but it doesn’t really stop them. It’s like I can hear a quieter, more reasonable voice saying, “This is probably bigger than it needs to be,” but the anxious part is much louder and says, “No, you have to keep thinking about it because something could go wrong.”

And it’s not just one topic anymore. Over this past year, it’s gone from mostly being about my mother’s safety to… finances, decisions I’ve made with staff, deadlines, things with the house, and then this constant question of whether I’m being a good daughter. So when a small thing comes up, like something I haven’t finished or something I need to double-check, it can suddenly feel like everything is about to unravel if I don’t get on top of it immediately.

So yes, even in the moment I sometimes know it’s too much, or at least that it doesn’t fully make sense. But it still feels urgent. That’s the hard part.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n6\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F42.9, F43.22, F60.5\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi… good to meet you too. I guess I came in because my worrying has gotten to the point where I can’t really put it away once it starts. Like, if I start worrying about my mother getting up at night, or a grant report I’m responsible for, or whether I missed some small thing at work, it just keeps going in my head.\n\nI make lists—honestly, I make a lot of lists—and I try to tell myself, “Okay, you’ve handled what you can, move on,” but it doesn’t really work. It’s like I can’t shelve the worry in my mind, which is embarrassing to say as a librarian, but that’s exactly what it feels like. It just stays checked out, so to speak, and I keep circling back to it no matter how much I try to redirect myself.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts ever feel intrusive and unwanted, like they pop into your mind even when you don’t want them to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a good way to put it. They feel unwanted in the sense that I’ll be trying to do something completely ordinary, like focus on a report at work or just get through the evening, and then the worry sort of appears and takes over. It isn’t like I’m choosing to sit down and think through it carefully. Once it starts—whether it’s about my mother getting up at night, or whether I’ve missed some detail at work, or the grant report—it’s very hard to push it aside.\n\nI’ll make lists, and then sometimes lists about the lists, trying to reassure myself that I’ve covered what I can. But even after that, my mind keeps circling back, like, “Are you sure? What if you forgot something?” So yes, they do feel intrusive, but mostly they feel… sticky, I guess. Like I can’t shelve them, no matter how much I tell myself to move on.\n\nDoctor: When these worries get stuck, do you find yourself doing anything over and over to feel less anxious, like checking, repeating, or going through something in your mind?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it repeating exactly… at least not in a ritual kind of way, if that’s what you mean. I don’t think I’m doing something like touching things a certain number of times or having to redo something in a very specific order.\n\nWhat I do notice is that I’ll go back over things in my head a lot, and I do check lists or rewrite them because it makes me feel like I’m at least trying to get the worry under control. But I’m not sure where the line is between “being responsible” and “doing it over and over because I’m anxious.” With work things especially, I’ll think, “Let me just make sure I didn’t miss anything,” and then sometimes I’ve spent much longer than I meant to just reviewing the same thing mentally.\n\nSo… yes and no, I guess. I do circle back and reassure myself, but I don’t know if it’s a compulsion exactly. It feels more like I’m trying to calm myself down and be certain, and then I can’t quite get the certainty I’m looking for.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about many different parts of your life, rather than mainly one specific problem?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It used to feel more centered on my mother—like whether she was safe at night, whether I should be doing more, whether I’d missed some sign that she needed help. But over this past year it’s spread out, and now it feels like almost any category of my life can turn into something I worry about.\n\nFinances, even if nothing immediate has happened. Staff decisions at work, like whether I handled a situation fairly or said the wrong thing. Deadlines, grant reports, house things I’ve been putting off, repairs, paperwork… and then this whole layer of, “Am I being a good daughter?” That one comes up a lot.\n\nAnd the frustrating thing is that even small things don’t stay small in my mind. Something like an email I haven’t answered or a bill I need to double-check can start to feel like it’s the first loose thread and everything is about to unravel. I know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it really feels like if I don’t catch every detail, something will go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your worrying is more than the situation really calls for?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, in the moment it feels completely justified, because my mind is telling me, “Well, if you don’t think this through, something bad could happen,” or “If you miss one detail, everything could unravel.” But when I step back, I can see that the level of worry is probably bigger than the actual situation.\n\nLike, it started mostly with my mother’s safety, and that felt understandable to me. But now it’s not just that. It’s finances, staff decisions at work, deadlines, the house, things I need to fix or follow up on, whether I’m being a good daughter… it’s like almost anything can become the thing I latch onto. And the size of the worry doesn’t always match the size of the problem. An unanswered email or a bill I need to double-check can start to feel like some huge warning sign, like if I don’t handle it perfectly, then everything is going to fall apart.\n\nSo yes, I do think it’s more than the situation calls for. I don’t always believe that when I’m in it, but afterward I can usually see that my reaction was bigger than what was actually happening.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts come up, do they feel like they don’t make sense or are excessive, even while you’re having them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think part of me knows they’re excessive, even while it’s happening, but it doesn’t really stop them. It’s like I can hear a quieter, more reasonable voice saying, “This is probably bigger than it needs to be,” but the anxious part is much louder and says, “No, you have to keep thinking about it because something could go wrong.”\n\nAnd it’s not just one topic anymore. Over this past year, it’s gone from mostly being about my mother’s safety to… finances, decisions I’ve made with staff, deadlines, things with the house, and then this constant question of whether I’m being a good daughter. So when a small thing comes up, like something I haven’t finished or something I need to double-check, it can suddenly feel like everything is about to unravel if I don’t get on top of it immediately.\n\nSo yes, even in the moment I sometimes know it’s too much, or at least that it doesn’t fully make sense. That’s the hard part.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. So when a small thing comes up, like something I haven’t finished or something I need to double-check, it can suddenly feel like everything is about to unravel if I don’t get on top of it immediately.\n\nSo yes, even in the moment I sometimes know it’s too much, or at least that it doesn’t fully make sense. But it still feels urgent. That’s the hard part.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you generally seen your need for order, lists, and getting things exactly right as just part of your personality, even when you weren’t especially anxious?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 47 / librarian_or_media_collections_specialist / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure, honestly. I can see that I’ve always liked things to be organized, especially with work—lists, systems, knowing where things are—but I don’t know if I’d say I’ve always needed things to be exactly right in a rigid way.

I mean, being a librarian, there’s a certain amount of order that just feels natural, or even necessary. I’m used to tracking details and making lists and having categories for things, so it’s hard for me to separate what’s just my work style or personality from what might be something more. I’ve probably always been someone who feels better when there’s a plan, but I don’t know that I noticed it as a problem when I wasn’t anxious.

So… I guess my answer is maybe, but I’m not completely sure what counts. It didn’t stand out to me as something extreme before, if that makes sense.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi… good to meet you too. I guess I came in because my worrying has gotten to the point where I can’t really put it away once it starts. Like, if I start worrying about my mother getting up at night, or a grant report I’m responsible for, or whether I missed some small thing at work, it just keeps going in my head.\n\nI make lists—honestly, I make a lot of lists—and I try to tell myself, “Okay, you’ve handled what you can, move on,” but it doesn’t really work. It’s like I can’t shelve the worry in my mind, which is embarrassing to say as a librarian, but that’s exactly what it feels like. It just stays checked out, so to speak, and I keep circling back to it no matter how much I try to redirect myself.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts ever feel intrusive and unwanted, like they pop into your mind even when you don’t want them to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a good way to put it. They feel unwanted in the sense that I’ll be trying to do something completely ordinary, like focus on a report at work or just get through the evening, and then the worry sort of appears and takes over. It isn’t like I’m choosing to sit down and think through it carefully. Once it starts—whether it’s about my mother getting up at night, or whether I’ve missed some detail at work, or the grant report—it’s very hard to push it aside.\n\nI’ll make lists, and then sometimes lists about the lists, trying to reassure myself that I’ve covered what I can. But even after that, my mind keeps circling back, like, “Are you sure? What if you forgot something?” So yes, they do feel intrusive, but mostly they feel… sticky, I guess. Like I can’t shelve them, no matter how much I tell myself to move on.\n\nDoctor: When these worries get stuck, do you find yourself doing anything over and over to feel less anxious, like checking, repeating, or going through something in your mind?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it repeating exactly… at least not in a ritual kind of way, if that’s what you mean. I don’t think I’m doing something like touching things a certain number of times or having to redo something in a very specific order.\n\nWhat I do notice is that I’ll go back over things in my head a lot, and I do check lists or rewrite them because it makes me feel like I’m at least trying to get the worry under control. But I’m not sure where the line is between “being responsible” and “doing it over and over because I’m anxious.” With work things especially, I’ll think, “Let me just make sure I didn’t miss anything,” and then sometimes I’ve spent much longer than I meant to just reviewing the same thing mentally.\n\nSo… yes and no, I guess. I do circle back and reassure myself, but I don’t know if it’s a compulsion exactly. It feels more like I’m trying to calm myself down and be certain, and then I can’t quite get the certainty I’m looking for.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about many different parts of your life, rather than mainly one specific problem?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It used to feel more centered on my mother—like whether she was safe at night, whether I should be doing more, whether I’d missed some sign that she needed help. But over this past year it’s spread out, and now it feels like almost any category of my life can turn into something I worry about.\n\nFinances, even if nothing immediate has happened. Staff decisions at work, like whether I handled a situation fairly or said the wrong thing. Deadlines, grant reports, house things I’ve been putting off, repairs, paperwork… and then this whole layer of, “Am I being a good daughter?” That one comes up a lot.\n\nAnd the frustrating thing is that even small things don’t stay small in my mind. Something like an email I haven’t answered or a bill I need to double-check can start to feel like it’s the first loose thread and everything is about to unravel. I know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it really feels like if I don’t catch every detail, something will go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your worrying is more than the situation really calls for?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, in the moment it feels completely justified, because my mind is telling me, “Well, if you don’t think this through, something bad could happen,” or “If you miss one detail, everything could unravel.” But when I step back, I can see that the level of worry is probably bigger than the actual situation.\n\nLike, it started mostly with my mother’s safety, and that felt understandable to me. But now it’s not just that. It’s finances, staff decisions at work, deadlines, the house, things I need to fix or follow up on, whether I’m being a good daughter… it’s like almost anything can become the thing I latch onto. And the size of the worry doesn’t always match the size of the problem. An unanswered email or a bill I need to double-check can start to feel like some huge warning sign, like if I don’t handle it perfectly, then everything is going to fall apart.\n\nSo yes, I do think it’s more than the situation calls for. I don’t always believe that when I’m in it, but afterward I can usually see that my reaction was bigger than what was actually happening.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts come up, do they feel like they don’t make sense or are excessive, even while you’re having them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think part of me knows they’re excessive, even while it’s happening, but it doesn’t really stop them. It’s like I can hear a quieter, more reasonable voice saying, “This is probably bigger than it needs to be,” but the anxious part is much louder and says, “No, you have to keep thinking about it because something could go wrong.”\n\nAnd it’s not just one topic anymore. Over this past year, it’s gone from mostly being about my mother’s safety to… finances, decisions I’ve made with staff, deadlines, things with the house, and then this constant question of whether I’m being a good daughter. So when a small thing comes up, like something I haven’t finished or something I need to double-check, it can suddenly feel like everything is about to unravel if I don’t get on top of it immediately.\n\nSo yes, even in the moment I sometimes know it’s too much, or at least that it doesn’t fully make sense. I’m used to tracking details and making lists and having categories for things, so it’s hard for me to separate what’s just my work style or personality from what might be something more. I don’t always believe that when I’m in it, but afterward I can usually see that my reaction was bigger than what was actually happening.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts come up, do they feel like they don’t make sense or are excessive, even while you’re having them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think part of me knows they’re excessive, even while it’s happening, but it doesn’t really stop them. It’s like I can hear a quieter, more reasonable voice saying, “This is probably bigger than it needs to be,” but the anxious part is much louder and says, “No, you have to keep thinking about it because something could go wrong.”\n\nAnd it’s not just one topic anymore. Over this past year, it’s gone from mostly being about my mother’s safety to… finances, decisions I’ve made with staff, deadlines, things with the house, and then this constant question of whether I’m being a good daughter. So when a small thing comes up, like something I haven’t finished or something I need to double-check, it can suddenly feel like everything is about to unravel if I don’t get on top of it immediately.\n\nSo yes, even in the moment I sometimes know it’s too much, or at least that it doesn’t fully make sense. But it still feels urgent. That’s the hard part.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally seen your need for order, lists, and getting things exactly right as just part of your personality, even when you weren’t especially anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I can see that I’ve always liked things to be organized, especially with work—lists, systems, knowing where things are—but I don’t know if I’d say I’ve always needed things to be exactly right in a rigid way.\n\nI mean, being a librarian, there’s a certain amount of order that just feels natural, or even necessary. I’m used to tracking details and making lists and having categories for things, so it’s hard for me to separate what’s just my work style or personality from what might be something more. I’ve probably always been someone who feels better when there’s a plan, but I don’t know that I noticed it as a problem when I wasn’t anxious.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is maybe, but I’m not completely sure what counts. It didn’t stand out to me as something extreme before, if that makes sense.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid this increase in worrying begin after a specific stressful event or change in your life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 47 / librarian_or_media_collections_specialist / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been going on for about a year and two months, since my mother moved into my home after her fall.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. It really seemed to start after my mother fell and then moved into my home. That was… about a year and two months ago now, I think. It’s strange because at first I saw it as just a normal reaction—she’d had this scary fall, and suddenly my house and my routines and my responsibilities all changed at once.

Before that, I worried about things, of course, but not like this. After she came to live with me, it felt like there was always something I needed to anticipate or prevent. I kept thinking, “What if she falls again?” or “What if I miss something?” And then over time the worry sort of spread out from there. But if I had to point to when it really increased, yes, it was when she moved in after the fall, about fourteen months ago.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F42.9, F43.22, F60.5\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi… good to meet you too. I guess I came in because my worrying has gotten to the point where I can’t really put it away once it starts. Like, if I start worrying about my mother getting up at night, or a grant report I’m responsible for, or whether I missed some small thing at work, it just keeps going in my head.\n\nI make lists—honestly, I make a lot of lists—and I try to tell myself, “Okay, you’ve handled what you can, move on,” but it doesn’t really work. It’s like I can’t shelve the worry in my mind, which is embarrassing to say as a librarian, but that’s exactly what it feels like. It just stays checked out, so to speak, and I keep circling back to it no matter how much I try to redirect myself.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts ever feel intrusive and unwanted, like they pop into your mind even when you don’t want them to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a good way to put it. They feel unwanted in the sense that I’ll be trying to do something completely ordinary, like focus on a report at work or just get through the evening, and then the worry sort of appears and takes over. It isn’t like I’m choosing to sit down and think through it carefully. Once it starts—whether it’s about my mother getting up at night, or whether I’ve missed some detail at work, or the grant report—it’s very hard to push it aside.\n\nI’ll make lists, and then sometimes lists about the lists, trying to reassure myself that I’ve covered what I can. But even after that, my mind keeps circling back, like, “Are you sure? What if you forgot something?” So yes, they do feel intrusive, but mostly they feel… sticky, I guess. Like I can’t shelve them, no matter how much I tell myself to move on.\n\nDoctor: When these worries get stuck, do you find yourself doing anything over and over to feel less anxious, like checking, repeating, or going through something in your mind?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it repeating exactly… at least not in a ritual kind of way, if that’s what you mean. I don’t think I’m doing something like touching things a certain number of times or having to redo something in a very specific order.\n\nWhat I do notice is that I’ll go back over things in my head a lot, and I do check lists or rewrite them because it makes me feel like I’m at least trying to get the worry under control. But I’m not sure where the line is between “being responsible” and “doing it over and over because I’m anxious.” With work things especially, I’ll think, “Let me just make sure I didn’t miss anything,” and then sometimes I’ve spent much longer than I meant to just reviewing the same thing mentally.\n\nSo… yes and no, I guess. I do circle back and reassure myself, but I don’t know if it’s a compulsion exactly. It feels more like I’m trying to calm myself down and be certain, and then I can’t quite get the certainty I’m looking for.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about many different parts of your life, rather than mainly one specific problem?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It used to feel more centered on my mother—like whether she was safe at night, whether I should be doing more, whether I’d missed some sign that she needed help. But over this past year it’s spread out, and now it feels like almost any category of my life can turn into something I worry about.\n\nFinances, even if nothing immediate has happened. Staff decisions at work, like whether I handled a situation fairly or said the wrong thing. Deadlines, grant reports, house things I’ve been putting off, repairs, paperwork… and then this whole layer of, “Am I being a good daughter?” That one comes up a lot.\n\nAnd the frustrating thing is that even small things don’t stay small in my mind. Something like an email I haven’t answered or a bill I need to double-check can start to feel like it’s the first loose thread and everything is about to unravel. I know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it really feels like if I don’t catch every detail, something will go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your worrying is more than the situation really calls for?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, in the moment it feels completely justified, because my mind is telling me, “Well, if you don’t think this through, something bad could happen,” or “If you miss one detail, everything could unravel.” But when I step back, I can see that the level of worry is probably bigger than the actual situation.\n\nLike, it started mostly with my mother’s safety, and that felt understandable to me. But now it’s not just that. It’s finances, staff decisions at work, deadlines, the house, things I need to fix or follow up on, whether I’m being a good daughter… it’s like almost anything can become the thing I latch onto. And the size of the worry doesn’t always match the size of the problem. An unanswered email or a bill I need to double-check can start to feel like some huge warning sign, like if I don’t handle it perfectly, then everything is going to fall apart.\n\nSo yes, I do think it’s more than the situation calls for. I don’t always believe that when I’m in it, but afterward I can usually see that my reaction was bigger than what was actually happening.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts come up, do they feel like they don’t make sense or are excessive, even while you’re having them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think part of me knows they’re excessive, even while it’s happening, but it doesn’t really stop them. It’s like I can hear a quieter, more reasonable voice saying, “This is probably bigger than it needs to be,” but the anxious part is much louder and says, “No, you have to keep thinking about it because something could go wrong.”\n\nAnd it’s not just one topic anymore. Over this past year, it’s gone from mostly being about my mother’s safety to… finances, decisions I’ve made with staff, deadlines, things with the house, and then this constant question of whether I’m being a good daughter. So when a small thing comes up, like something I haven’t finished or something I need to double-check, it can suddenly feel like everything is about to unravel if I don’t get on top of it immediately.\n\nSo yes, even in the moment I sometimes know it’s too much, or at least that it doesn’t fully make sense. But it still feels urgent. That’s the hard part.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally seen your need for order, lists, and getting things exactly right as just part of your personality, even when you weren’t especially anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I can see that I’ve always liked things to be organized, especially with work—lists, systems, knowing where things are—but I don’t know if I’d say I’ve always needed things to be exactly right in a rigid way.\n\nI mean, being a librarian, there’s a certain amount of order that just feels natural, or even necessary. I’m used to tracking details and making lists and having categories for things, so it’s hard for me to separate what’s just my work style or personality from what might be something more. I’ve probably always been someone who feels better when there’s a plan, but I don’t know that I noticed it as a problem when I wasn’t anxious.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is maybe, but I’m not completely sure what counts. It didn’t stand out to me as something extreme before, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did this increase in worrying begin after a specific stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes. It really seemed to start after my mother fell and then moved into my home. That was… about a year and two months ago now, I think. It’s strange because at first I saw it as just a normal reaction—she’d had this scary fall, and suddenly my house and my routines and my responsibilities all changed at once.\n\nBefore that, I worried about things, of course, but not like this. After she came to live with me, it felt like there was always something I needed to anticipate or prevent. I kept thinking, “What if she falls again?” or “What if I miss something?” And then over time the worry sort of spread out from there. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi… good to meet you too. I guess I came in because my worrying has gotten to the point where I can’t really put it away once it starts. Like, if I start worrying about my mother getting up at night, or a grant report I’m responsible for, or whether I missed some small thing at work, it just keeps going in my head.\n\nI make lists—honestly, I make a lot of lists—and I try to tell myself, “Okay, you’ve handled what you can, move on,” but it doesn’t really work. It’s like I can’t shelve the worry in my mind, which is embarrassing to say as a librarian, but that’s exactly what it feels like. It just stays checked out, so to speak, and I keep circling back to it no matter how much I try to redirect myself.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts ever feel intrusive and unwanted, like they pop into your mind even when you don’t want them to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a good way to put it. They feel unwanted in the sense that I’ll be trying to do something completely ordinary, like focus on a report at work or just get through the evening, and then the worry sort of appears and takes over. It isn’t like I’m choosing to sit down and think through it carefully. Once it starts—whether it’s about my mother getting up at night, or whether I’ve missed some detail at work, or the grant report—it’s very hard to push it aside.\n\nI’ll make lists, and then sometimes lists about the lists, trying to reassure myself that I’ve covered what I can. But even after that, my mind keeps circling back, like, “Are you sure? What if you forgot something?” So yes, they do feel intrusive, but mostly they feel… sticky, I guess. Like I can’t shelve them, no matter how much I tell myself to move on.\n\nDoctor: When these worries get stuck, do you find yourself doing anything over and over to feel less anxious, like checking, repeating, or going through something in your mind?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it repeating exactly… at least not in a ritual kind of way, if that’s what you mean. I don’t think I’m doing something like touching things a certain number of times or having to redo something in a very specific order.\n\nWhat I do notice is that I’ll go back over things in my head a lot, and I do check lists or rewrite them because it makes me feel like I’m at least trying to get the worry under control. But I’m not sure where the line is between “being responsible” and “doing it over and over because I’m anxious.” With work things especially, I’ll think, “Let me just make sure I didn’t miss anything,” and then sometimes I’ve spent much longer than I meant to just reviewing the same thing mentally.\n\nSo… yes and no, I guess. I do circle back and reassure myself, but I don’t know if it’s a compulsion exactly. It feels more like I’m trying to calm myself down and be certain, and then I can’t quite get the certainty I’m looking for.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about many different parts of your life, rather than mainly one specific problem?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It used to feel more centered on my mother—like whether she was safe at night, whether I should be doing more, whether I’d missed some sign that she needed help. But over this past year it’s spread out, and now it feels like almost any category of my life can turn into something I worry about.\n\nFinances, even if nothing immediate has happened. Staff decisions at work, like whether I handled a situation fairly or said the wrong thing. Deadlines, grant reports, house things I’ve been putting off, repairs, paperwork… and then this whole layer of, “Am I being a good daughter?” That one comes up a lot.\n\nAnd the frustrating thing is that even small things don’t stay small in my mind. Something like an email I haven’t answered or a bill I need to double-check can start to feel like it’s the first loose thread and everything is about to unravel. I know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it really feels like if I don’t catch every detail, something will go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your worrying is more than the situation really calls for?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, in the moment it feels completely justified, because my mind is telling me, “Well, if you don’t think this through, something bad could happen,” or “If you miss one detail, everything could unravel.” But when I step back, I can see that the level of worry is probably bigger than the actual situation.\n\nLike, it started mostly with my mother’s safety, and that felt understandable to me. But now it’s not just that. It’s finances, staff decisions at work, deadlines, the house, things I need to fix or follow up on, whether I’m being a good daughter… it’s like almost anything can become the thing I latch onto. And the size of the worry doesn’t always match the size of the problem. An unanswered email or a bill I need to double-check can start to feel like some huge warning sign, like if I don’t handle it perfectly, then everything is going to fall apart.\n\nSo yes, I do think it’s more than the situation calls for. I don’t always believe that when I’m in it, but afterward I can usually see that my reaction was bigger than what was actually happening.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts come up, do they feel like they don’t make sense or are excessive, even while you’re having them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think part of me knows they’re excessive, even while it’s happening, but it doesn’t really stop them. It’s like I can hear a quieter, more reasonable voice saying, “This is probably bigger than it needs to be,” but the anxious part is much louder and says, “No, you have to keep thinking about it because something could go wrong.”\n\nAnd it’s not just one topic anymore. Over this past year, it’s gone from mostly being about my mother’s safety to… finances, decisions I’ve made with staff, deadlines, things with the house, and then this constant question of whether I’m being a good daughter. So when a small thing comes up, like something I haven’t finished or something I need to double-check, it can suddenly feel like everything is about to unravel if I don’t get on top of it immediately.\n\nSo yes, even in the moment I sometimes know it’s too much, or at least that it doesn’t fully make sense. shoulders feel locked up from bracing myself, and I catch myself clenching my jaw while answering emails or listening for my mother in the next room.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional me and has made me worry about whether I can keep leading my team well.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 2 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about a year and two months, since my mother moved into my home after her fall.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 47 / librarian_or_media_collections_specialist / graduate\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S057\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you generally seen your need for order, lists, and getting things exactly right as just part of your personality, even when you weren’t especially anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I can see that I’ve always liked things to be organized, especially with work—lists, systems, knowing where things are—but I don’t know if I’d say I’ve always needed things to be exactly right in a rigid way.\n\nI mean, being a librarian, there’s a certain amount of order that just feels natural, or even necessary. I’m used to tracking details and making lists and having categories for things, so it’s hard for me to separate what’s just my work style or personality from what might be something more. I’ve probably always been someone who feels better when there’s a plan, but I don’t know that I noticed it as a problem when I wasn’t anxious.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is maybe, but I’m not completely sure what counts. It didn’t stand out to me as something extreme before, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did this increase in worrying begin after a specific stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes. It really seemed to start after my mother fell and then moved into my home. That was… about a year and two months ago now, I think. It’s strange because at first I saw it as just a normal reaction—she’d had this scary fall, and suddenly my house and my routines and my responsibilities all changed at once.\n\nBefore that, I worried about things, of course, but not like this. After she came to live with me, it felt like there was always something I needed to anticipate or prevent. I kept thinking, “What if she falls again?” or “What if I miss something?” And then over time the worry sort of spread out from there. But if I had to point to when it really increased, yes, it was when she moved in after the fall, about fourteen months ago.\n\nDoctor: Has this level of worrying been present most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely most days, and for longer than six months. It’s been about a year and two months now, really since my mother moved into my home after her fall.\n\nAt first I kept thinking it was just temporary, like I was adjusting to a big change, but it hasn’t really let up in the way I expected. So yes, if you’re asking whether this level of worry has been there most days for at least six months, I’d say yes—more like fourteen months.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you go back over lists or review things in your mind, does it feel like you have to do it to relieve the anxiety, even when you know it’s excessive?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 10\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }

I can see that I’ve always liked things to be organized, especially with work—lists, systems, knowing where things are—but I don’t know if I’d say I’ve always needed things to be exactly right in a rigid way.\n\nI mean, being a librarian, there’s a certain amount of order that just feels natural, or even necessary. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I do go back over lists and things in my head, yes, but I don’t know if it feels like a “have to” in the way you mean.

Sometimes it’s more like… if I don’t review it, I feel unsettled, like I might be missing something important. So I’ll look again, or mentally walk through what needs to happen, and it can make me feel a little calmer for the moment. But I’m not always aware of it as, “I’m doing this to relieve anxiety.” It just feels practical at the time, like I’m trying to be responsible or make sure nothing slips through.

I can also tell that sometimes it’s probably more than necessary, especially when I’ve already checked and I’m still thinking about it. But I don’t know that I’d describe it as a ritual exactly. It’s more this nagging sense that reviewing things will help me feel more prepared, even if part of me knows I may be overdoing it.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F42.9, F43.22, F60.5\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi… good to meet you too. I guess I came in because my worrying has gotten to the point where I can’t really put it away once it starts. Like, if I start worrying about my mother getting up at night, or a grant report I’m responsible for, or whether I missed some small thing at work, it just keeps going in my head.\n\nI make lists—honestly, I make a lot of lists—and I try to tell myself, “Okay, you’ve handled what you can, move on,” but it doesn’t really work. It’s like I can’t shelve the worry in my mind, which is embarrassing to say as a librarian, but that’s exactly what it feels like. It just stays checked out, so to speak, and I keep circling back to it no matter how much I try to redirect myself.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts ever feel intrusive and unwanted, like they pop into your mind even when you don’t want them to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a good way to put it. They feel unwanted in the sense that I’ll be trying to do something completely ordinary, like focus on a report at work or just get through the evening, and then the worry sort of appears and takes over. It isn’t like I’m choosing to sit down and think through it carefully. Once it starts—whether it’s about my mother getting up at night, or whether I’ve missed some detail at work, or the grant report—it’s very hard to push it aside.\n\nI’ll make lists, and then sometimes lists about the lists, trying to reassure myself that I’ve covered what I can. But even after that, my mind keeps circling back, like, “Are you sure? What if you forgot something?” So yes, they do feel intrusive, but mostly they feel… sticky, I guess. Like I can’t shelve them, no matter how much I tell myself to move on.\n\nDoctor: When these worries get stuck, do you find yourself doing anything over and over to feel less anxious, like checking, repeating, or going through something in your mind?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it repeating exactly… at least not in a ritual kind of way, if that’s what you mean. I don’t think I’m doing something like touching things a certain number of times or having to redo something in a very specific order.\n\nWhat I do notice is that I’ll go back over things in my head a lot, and I do check lists or rewrite them because it makes me feel like I’m at least trying to get the worry under control. But I’m not sure where the line is between “being responsible” and “doing it over and over because I’m anxious.” With work things especially, I’ll think, “Let me just make sure I didn’t miss anything,” and then sometimes I’ve spent much longer than I meant to just reviewing the same thing mentally.\n\nSo… yes and no, I guess. I do circle back and reassure myself, but I don’t know if it’s a compulsion exactly. It feels more like I’m trying to calm myself down and be certain, and then I can’t quite get the certainty I’m looking for.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about many different parts of your life, rather than mainly one specific problem?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It used to feel more centered on my mother—like whether she was safe at night, whether I should be doing more, whether I’d missed some sign that she needed help. But over this past year it’s spread out, and now it feels like almost any category of my life can turn into something I worry about.\n\nFinances, even if nothing immediate has happened. Staff decisions at work, like whether I handled a situation fairly or said the wrong thing. Deadlines, grant reports, house things I’ve been putting off, repairs, paperwork… and then this whole layer of, “Am I being a good daughter?” That one comes up a lot.\n\nAnd the frustrating thing is that even small things don’t stay small in my mind. Something like an email I haven’t answered or a bill I need to double-check can start to feel like it’s the first loose thread and everything is about to unravel. I know that sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it really feels like if I don’t catch every detail, something will go wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your worrying is more than the situation really calls for?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, in the moment it feels completely justified, because my mind is telling me, “Well, if you don’t think this through, something bad could happen,” or “If you miss one detail, everything could unravel.” But when I step back, I can see that the level of worry is probably bigger than the actual situation.\n\nLike, it started mostly with my mother’s safety, and that felt understandable to me. But now it’s not just that. It’s finances, staff decisions at work, deadlines, the house, things I need to fix or follow up on, whether I’m being a good daughter… it’s like almost anything can become the thing I latch onto. And the size of the worry doesn’t always match the size of the problem. An unanswered email or a bill I need to double-check can start to feel like some huge warning sign, like if I don’t handle it perfectly, then everything is going to fall apart.\n\nSo yes, I do think it’s more than the situation calls for. I don’t always believe that when I’m in it, but afterward I can usually see that my reaction was bigger than what was actually happening.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts come up, do they feel like they don’t make sense or are excessive, even while you’re having them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think part of me knows they’re excessive, even while it’s happening, but it doesn’t really stop them. It’s like I can hear a quieter, more reasonable voice saying, “This is probably bigger than it needs to be,” but the anxious part is much louder and says, “No, you have to keep thinking about it because something could go wrong.”\n\nAnd it’s not just one topic anymore. Over this past year, it’s gone from mostly being about my mother’s safety to… finances, decisions I’ve made with staff, deadlines, things with the house, and then this constant question of whether I’m being a good daughter. So when a small thing comes up, like something I haven’t finished or something I need to double-check, it can suddenly feel like everything is about to unravel if I don’t get on top of it immediately.\n\nSo yes, even in the moment I sometimes know it’s too much, or at least that it doesn’t fully make sense. But it still feels urgent. That’s the hard part.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally seen your need for order, lists, and getting things exactly right as just part of your personality, even when you weren’t especially anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I can see that I’ve always liked things to be organized, especially with work—lists, systems, knowing where things are—but I don’t know if I’d say I’ve always needed things to be exactly right in a rigid way.\n\nI mean, being a librarian, there’s a certain amount of order that just feels natural, or even necessary. I’m used to tracking details and making lists and having categories for things, so it’s hard for me to separate what’s just my work style or personality from what might be something more. I’ve probably always been someone who feels better when there’s a plan, but I don’t know that I noticed it as a problem when I wasn’t anxious.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is maybe, but I’m not completely sure what counts. It didn’t stand out to me as something extreme before, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did this increase in worrying begin after a specific stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yes. It really seemed to start after my mother fell and then moved into my home. That was… about a year and two months ago now, I think. It’s strange because at first I saw it as just a normal reaction—she’d had this scary fall, and suddenly my house and my routines and my responsibilities all changed at once.\n\nBefore that, I worried about things, of course, but not like this. After she came to live with me, it felt like there was always something I needed to anticipate or prevent. I kept thinking, “What if she falls again?” or “What if I miss something?” And then over time the worry sort of spread out from there. But if I had to point to when it really increased, yes, it was when she moved in after the fall, about fourteen months ago.\n\nDoctor: Has this level of worrying been present most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely most days, and for longer than six months. It’s been about a year and two months now, really since my mother moved into my home after her fall.\n\nAt first I kept thinking it was just temporary, like I was adjusting to a big change, but it hasn’t really let up in the way I expected. So yes, if you’re asking whether this level of worry has been there most days for at least six months, I’d say yes—more like fourteen months.\n\nDoctor: When you go back over lists or review things in your mind, does it feel like you have to do it to relieve the anxiety, even when you know it’s excessive?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I do go back over lists and things in my head, yes, but I don’t know if it feels like a “have to” in the way you mean.\n\nSometimes it’s more like… if I don’t review it, I feel unsettled, like I might be missing something important. So I’ll look again, or mentally walk through what needs to happen, and it can make me feel a little calmer for the moment. But I’m not always aware of it as, “I’m doing this to relieve anxiety.” It just feels practical at the time, like I’m trying to be responsible or make sure nothing slips through.\n\nI can also tell that sometimes it’s probably more than necessary, especially when I’ve already checked and I’m still thinking about it. But I don’t know that I’d describe it as a ritual exactly. It’s more this nagging sense that reviewing things will help me feel more prepared, even if part of me knows I may be overdoing it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think that’s exactly what it feels like, even if I don’t always say it to myself that directly. It’s not usually some very specific belief like, “If I don’t check this list, then this exact terrible thing will happen,” but it’s more this awful feeling that if I don’t go back over it, I’ll have missed something important and then something will go wrong.

Like with my mother, if I don’t mentally review whether she has what she needs, or whether I heard her get up, or whether something is in the right place, I start imagining that maybe she’ll get up at night and I won’t notice, or I’ll have failed to anticipate something. And at work it’s similar—if I don’t go back over a grant report or some detail in a project, I get stuck on the idea that I may have overlooked something and it’ll create a problem later.

The frustrating thing is, even when I do make lists or check things, the worry doesn’t really stay put. Once it starts, I can’t seem to shelve it in my mind, which is ironic given my job. I can try to redirect myself, or tell myself I already checked, but it keeps coming back around. So yes, I guess I do feel like not reviewing means I’m leaving the door open for something bad to happen, even if I also know I may be overdoing it.

I mean, in the moment it feels completely justified, because my mind is telling me, “Well, if you don’t think this through, something bad could happen,” or “If you miss one detail, everything could unravel.” But when I step back, I can see that the level of worry is probably bigger than the actual situation.\n\nLike, it started mostly with my mother’s safety, and that felt understandable to me.
